> We should explain the distinction between **authentication** and **commitment**:
> - Authentication answers "is this the authorized person?" The user is the authorized person; the PIN doesn't distinguish them from an impulsive version of themselves.
> - A commitment device needs friction that the present self cannot instantly undo: delays, partner approval, off-device secrets, or consequences.
> - Therefore, the same PIN is fine for low-stakes unlock (it's a speed bump), but not for high-stakes disable. The problem isn't reuse per se; it's that the same easy secret can undo the whole system.
> 
> A good plan:
> 1. **Separate the two roles**:
>    - Unlock PIN (4–6 digits): 5-min extension, 3/day. User-set, hashed, never displayed.
>    - Admin/recovery key (long random code or partner): turning off, changing allowance, changing PIN. Not memorized; stored off-device or held by partner. This makes the high-impact actions genuinely hard.
>    - Alternatively, no admin key: use a **24-hour delay** for disabling/changing the limit. This is simpler and very effective for self-control. The user can still change their mind, but not in the moment.
> 2. **Add attempt throttling** to the unlock PIN: 3 wrong → 30s, 6 wrong → 5 min, 10 wrong → 1h. Since it's 4 digits, brute force is trivial otherwise.
> 3. **Stop hardcoding**: set the PIN at first run; store a salted hash (PBKDF2) in prefs; never log it (the current code logs "PIN check for 4 digits -> accepted" but not the value; okay). Use a 6-digit PIN to make guessing harder. A 4-digit PIN with throttling is fine for friction.
> 4. **Reconsider the pause**: a 15-min pause for one extension is generous; either remove it or price it at 2–3 extensions. Or replace "3 times" with a **15-minute daily extension budget**, 5 min per unlock; pause costs 15. That's a cleaner model.
> 5. **Long-term**: partner/backend for real accountability. The previous WhatsApp code flow prefilled the code, so it wasn't secure; a backend that sends the code to the partner only after approval is the real fix.
> 
> Recommendation:
> - For the current prototype: **keep one PIN for unlocks, but stop using it for admin actions.** Implement a **cooling-off period** for disable/limit changes (e.g., 10 min for limit, 24h for disable) and a **separate 6-digit admin PIN** or recovery code. Add attempt throttling. This is the smallest change that materially improves strictness.
> - If they want the simplest: user-set 6-digit PIN + 3/day + throttle + 24h delay on disable/limit; no second PIN. The "same PIN" is then acceptable because the high-impact actions are delay-gated, not just PIN-gated.
> 
> Should we implement anything now? The user asks "is that okay or can we come up with any better plan?" — answer, don't code. We can propose and ask which. The instructions say open-ended/high-level: settle scope before building. So no code changes. Good.
> 
> Need to be careful not to overstate security. A 4-digit PIN stored as a hash can be brute-forced offline if someone has the device/root; but the threat is the user, not an attacker. A partner/backend is the only real accountability. A cooling-off period is the best on-device commitment mechanism. Mention that.

Short answer: **one PIN is fine as a speed bump, but it is not a real lock while the same PIN can instantly turn the blocker off or raise the limit.** The problem is not reuse itself — it is that the high-impact actions are protected by the same easily-known secret.

Right now the weak points are concrete:

- `BlockPolicy.PIN = "2310"` is hardcoded, so it is recoverable from the APK and identical on every install.
- A 4-digit PIN is only 10,000 combinations, and there is no wrong-attempt throttle.
- The same PIN unlocks Instagram, pauses for 15 minutes, changes the daily allowance, and turns the blocker off. So the 3-per-day cap is bypassable in two taps by turning the blocker off or raising the limit.
- On-device, a determined owner can still clear app data or uninstall. No local PIN can fully prevent that.

## A better plan

**Option A — one PIN + cooling-off (recommended, smallest change)**

- Let the user set a 6-digit PIN on first run; store a salted hash in `SharedPreferences`, never display it, remove the hardcoded `2310`.
- Keep that PIN for the 5-minute unlock and 15-minute pause, still 3 times a day.
- Add wrong-PIN throttling: 3 failures → 30 s, 6 → 5 min.
- Make the destructive actions **time-delayed**:
  - Change allowance: takes effect after ~10 minutes.
  - Turn off the blocker: takes effect after ~24 hours.
  - Show a pending banner with a Cancel button; the blocker stays active until the delay expires.

This is the key point: **reusing one PIN is acceptable if the dangerous actions are delay-gated.** The delay is the commitment device, not the PIN.

**Option B — two roles, no delay**

- Unlock PIN (6 digits) for the 5-minute/15-minute sessions.
- Generate a random 8+ character **recovery/admin key** at setup, show it once, store only its hash, and tell the user to keep it off the phone.
- Turning off the blocker, changing the allowance, or changing the PIN requires that key. The unlock PIN alone cannot disable anything.

Do not add a second 4-digit PIN — it has the same guessability and is just one more thing to forget.

**Option C — real accountability**

The only genuinely strong model is someone else holding the authority: a partner approves a backend-sent code, or a device-owner/MDM policy prevents uninstall. The old WhatsApp flow was not real security because the code was pre-filled in the message the user could read.

## My recommendation

Go with **Option A** now: user-set 6-digit PIN, attempt throttling, 3 sessions/day, and cooling-off periods for turning off/changing the limit. It closes the “same PIN bypasses everything” problem without a backend or a second secret. If you want stronger separation without delays, do Option B instead.

One extra design point: the 15-minute pause currently costs the same as a 5-minute unlock. Consider pricing it at 2–3 extensions, or replacing “3 times” with a 15-minute daily extension budget.

Which direction do you want me to implement?
